-form symmetry generated by the following operator defined on a non-contractible 1-cycle
\gamma

W_{\gamma}=\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in\gamma}w_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{o}(\mathsf{e},\gamma)}\,,\quad w_{\mathsf{e}}=Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{\phantom{\dagger}}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{\dagger}\,.

(2.64)

Notice that the local representative of the line operator cannot be the naive choice
\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}
since it is not gauge invariant.
Furthermore
W_{L}
, for a contractible 1-cycle
L
, acts as the identity on the constrained (flux-free) Hilbert space.
Just like
\mathcal{U}
, it is unusual to think of
the line operators
W_{\gamma}
as
(d-1)
-form
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generators since they do not square to the identity depending on
\mathsf{g}
in (
2.62
).

In order to clarify the mixed anomaly, we need to define operators that measure the symmetry twisted boundary conditions for the 0-form and
(d-1)
-form symmetries.
As discussed in Sec.
2.1
, symmetry twisted boundary conditions with respect to the 0-form symmetry (
\mathsf{STBC}^{(0)}
) are related to the holonomy
\mathsf{g}(\gamma)
of a 1-form
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge field around any non-contractible 1-cycle
\gamma
.
Likewise the symmetry twisted boundary conditions with respect to the
d-1
-form symmetry (
\mathsf{STBC}^{(d-1)}
) correspond to a holonomy
\alpha
of a
d
-form
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
background gauge field around the fundamental
d
-cycle, i.e. around all of space.
These holonomies are measured by the operators
\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)}

\begin{split}\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma}&=\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in\gamma}Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{2}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{2}=(-1)^{\mathsf{g}(\gamma)}\,,\qquad\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)}=\prod_{\mathsf{v}}A_{\mathsf{v}}=(-1)^{\alpha}\,.\end{split}

(2.65)

A manifestation of the mixed-anomaly is that the 0-form
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry is fractionalized to
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
in the symmetry twisted sector of the
(d-1)
-form symmetry and conversely, the
(d-1)
-form symmetry is fractionalized to a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry in the symmetry twisted sector of the 0-form symmetry (see Fig.
7
)

\mathcal{U}^{2}=\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)}\,,\qquad W_{\gamma}^{2}=\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma}\,.

(2.66)

Figure 7
:
Figure (a) depicts the equation
W_{\gamma}^{2}=\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma}
in terms of symmetry defects. The symmetry twisted boundary conditions for
\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma}
is implemented by a codimension-1 operator (in green) that extends in the
d-1
homology dual to
\gamma
. Figure (b) depicts the
\mathcal{U}^{2}=\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)}
in terms of symmetry defects.

Another related consequence of the mixed anomaly is the symmetry fractionalization on the local representative of a
(d-1)
-form symmetry generator.
More precisely, the operator
W_{L}
defined on an open line
L
, takes the form

W_{L}=Z_{\mathsf{s}(L)}^{\phantom{\dagger}}\left[\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in L}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}\right]Z_{\mathsf{t}(L)}^{\dagger}\,.

(2.67)

Notice, that the end-points of the string are now appended with operators that are charged under
\mathcal{U}
and in fact carry a fractional charge, i.e., the
\mathcal{U}^{2}
eigenvalue is
-1
for such an operator (see Fig.
9
).
This is the phenomena of symmetry fractionalization
[
103
,
104
,
7
,
106
,
105
]
and is related to a mixed anomaly between the 0-form symmetry and
(d-1)
-form symmetry as we will describe in more detail in the next section.

Solving the Gauss constraint:

Let us now try to find a unitary that disentangles the edge degrees of freedom from the Gauss operator such that the Gauss constraint may be solved.
Specifically, we seek an operator
U
such that

U\,\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{v}}\,U^{\dagger}=X_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}\,.

(2.68)

We make an ansatz that the action is a generalized controlled operation, i.e., it acts as

U=\sum_{a,\phi}|a+f(\phi),\phi\rangle\langle a,\phi|\,,

(2.69)

Inserting the ansatz (
2.69
) into (
2.68
), one finds the constraint
f(\phi)+f(\phi+2\delta^{(\mathsf{v})})={\rm d}\delta^{(\mathsf{v})}
,
which can be solved by
f={\rm d}\lfloor\phi/2\rfloor
, where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
denoted the floor function.
Therefore the unitary is

U=\sum_{a,\phi}|a+{\rm d}\lfloor\phi/2\rfloor,\phi\rangle\langle a,\phi|\,.

(2.70)

Using (
2.70
), the action on all the remaining operators in the bond algebra can be computed.
For instance
\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{e}}
and
Z_{\mathsf{v}}
remain invariant under the action of
U
.
Meanwhile
X_{\mathsf{v}}
and
\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}
transform in a more involved way

\begin{split}UX_{\mathsf{v}}U^{\dagger}&=\sum_{a,\phi}\big|a+{\rm d}(\lfloor\phi/2\rfloor+\lfloor(\phi+\delta^{(\mathsf{v})})/2\rfloor),\phi+\delta^{(\mathsf{v})}\big\rangle\big\langle a,\phi\big|\\
&=X_{\mathsf{v}}\left[P_{\mathsf{v}}^{(+)}+A_{\mathsf{v}}P_{\mathsf{v}}^{(-)}\right]\,,\\
UZ^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}Z^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}U^{\dagger}&=\sum_{a,\phi}\exp\left\{{\rm i}\pi\left(a+{\rm d}\lfloor\phi/2\rfloor\right)_{\mathsf{e}}+\frac{{\rm i}\pi(\rm d\phi)_{\mathsf{e}}}{2}\right\}|a,\phi\rangle\langle a,\phi|\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-{\rm i}Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{2}\right)\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}\left(1+{\rm i}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{2}\right)\,,\end{split}

(2.71)

where we have defined
P^{(\pm)}_{\mathsf{v}}=(1\pm Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{2})/2
.
We are now in a position to write down the unitary transformed bond algebra

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}_{\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}}(\mathcal{V}_{\rm ext})&=\Big\langle X_{\mathsf{v}}\left[P_{\mathsf{v}}^{(+)}+A_{\mathsf{v}}P_{\mathsf{v}}^{(-)}\right]\,,\ \frac{1-{\rm i}Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}\frac{1+{\rm i}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\ \Big|\ X_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1\,,\ \\
&\quad\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in L}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1\,,\ \forall\ \mathsf{v}\,,\mathsf{e}\Big\rangle\,.\end{split}

(2.72)

Since the constraint has now been localized on the vertices, it can be readily solved.
We define a restricted basis on the vertex Hilbert space
\mathcal{V}^{\rm{rest.}}_{\mathsf{v}}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}
spanned by

\begin{split}|\uparrow\,\rangle&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big\{|\phi_{\mathsf{v}}=0\rangle+|\phi_{\mathsf{v}}=2\rangle\big\}\,,\\
|\downarrow\,\rangle&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big\{|\phi_{\mathsf{v}}=1\rangle+|\phi_{\mathsf{v}}=3\rangle\big\}\,,\end{split}

(2.73)

for which
X_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}=1
.

Figure 8
:
The operator
\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{v}}
acts in two steps.
In the first step, it measures the spin at vertex
\mathsf{v}
and implements a gauge transformation (via
A_{\mathsf{v}}
) on the neighboring edges depending on the measurement outcome.
In the second step, it flips the spin at
\mathsf{v}
.

The operators
X_{\mathsf{v}}
and
Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
acting on
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}
can be restricted to
\mathcal{V}^{\rm{rest.}}_{\mathsf{v}}
since these operators commute with
X_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
and therefore leave the space spanned by
2.73
invariant.
In this basis,

X_{\mathsf{v}}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}^{\rm{rest.}}_{\mathsf{v}}}\sim\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{v}}\,,\qquad Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}^{\rm{rest.}}_{\mathsf{v}}}\sim\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{v}}\,.

(2.74)

Since only combinations of
X_{\mathsf{v}}
and
Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{2}
appear in the bond algebra in (
2.72
), we can solve the Gauss constraint and directly work in the restricted Hilbert space

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}_{\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}}(\mathcal{V}^{\rm{rest.}})&=\Big\langle\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{v}}\left[P_{\mathsf{v}}^{(+)}+A_{\mathsf{v}}P_{\mathsf{v}}^{(-)}\right]\,,\frac{1-{\rm i}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}\frac{1+{\rm i}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}}{\sqrt{2}}\,\Big|\,\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in L}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1\ \forall\,\mathsf{v}\,,\mathsf{e}\Big\rangle\,,\end{split}

(2.75)

where
\mathcal{V}^{\rm{rest.}}=\bigotimes_{\mathsf{v}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}^{\rm{rest.}}\bigotimes_{\mathsf{e}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\rm{ext.}}
and
P^{(\pm)}_{\mathsf{v}}:=(1\pm\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{v}})/2
on
\mathcal{V}^{\rm{res.}}
.

Symmetry and mixed anomaly for the transformed bond algebra:

Since the bond algebras (
2.60
) and (
2.75
) are isomorphic, they have identical symmetry structures.
In the frame of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}_{\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}}
, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
0-form symmetry is generated by

\mathcal{U}=\prod_{\mathsf{v}}u_{\mathsf{v}}\,,\qquad u_{\mathsf{v}}=\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{v}}\left[P^{(+)}_{\mathsf{v}}+A_{\mathsf{v}}P^{(-)}_{\mathsf{v}}\right]\,

(2.76)

while the
(d-1)
-form symmetry generated by the (closed) line operator

W_{\gamma}=\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in\gamma}w_{\mathsf{e}}^{\mathsf{o}(\mathsf{e},\gamma)}\,,\qquad w_{\mathsf{e}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-{\rm i}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}\right)\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}\left(1+{\rm i}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}\right)\,.

(2.77)

These two symmetries have a mixed anomaly, which manifests as

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}^{2}&=\prod_{\mathsf{v}}A_{\mathsf{v}}=\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)}\,,\\
W_{\gamma}^{2}&=\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in\gamma}\sigma_{\mathsf{e}}^{z}=\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma}\,,\end{split}

(2.78)

and reproduces (
2.66
).
In the next sections, we will see that this anomaly has important consequences for the phase realized in the partially gauged model.

Figure 9
:
The end-point of a 1-form symmetry
W(L)
traps an operator which carries a fractional charge under the 0-form symmetry.
This symmetry fractionalization is a diagnostic of a mixed anomaly between the 0-form and 1-form symmetry.

3
Gauging as topological dualities

In this section, we describe a general procedure
[
65
]
to gauge a finite global symmetry and its sub-groups from a space-time point of view deriving relations between partition functions and energy spectra of dual theories.
We will pay attention to the global symmetry of the gauged theory thus obtained and to the mapping of the symmetry twisted sectors between the gauged and original theory.
As we described in Sec.
2
, when gauging a finite subgroup of the full symmetry group, the dual or gauged theory has a symmetry structure with a mixed anomaly.
In such cases, the mapping of symmetry sectors can be subtle.
We detail how this works for the simplest case of gauging
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, which contains the main new result of this section.
Although, we describe this specific simplest case, our analysis and approach generalize to other finite Abelian groups.

3.1
Gauging finite Abelian symmetry

We begin by describing the gauging of a finite Abelian symmetry group in a
d+1
dimensional quantum system
\mathfrak{T}
, before addressing its subgroups. Unlike the previous analysis working on the level of Hilbert spaces of spin models, here we take a space-time point of view and work on the level of partition functions.

3.1.1
Gauging, ungauging and dual symmetries

Let us consider a quantum system, denoted by
\mathfrak{T}
in
d+1
spacetime dimensions and symmetric under a finite Abelian group
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}
.
Such a theory can be defined in the presence of a background
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}
gauge field
A_{1}
, which can equivalently be understood as a network of codimension-1 (in spacetime) symmetry defects.
We denote the partition function of
\mathfrak{T}
coupled to a
A_{1}
background by
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}[A_{1}]
.
If the theory
\mathfrak{T}
does not have any ’t Hooft anomaly with respect to the group
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}
, then
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}
can be gauged in
\mathfrak{T}
to obtain a new theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
.
Gauging the 0-form symmetry amounts to summing over background gauge fields/symmetry defect networks
[
120
,
121
,
18
,
26
]
.
The partition function of the gauged theory has the form

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}=\frac{1}{{|G|^{b_{0}(M)}}}\sum_{a_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}[a_{1}]\,,

(3.1)

where we have assumed that
M
is path connected and the sum is over gauge classes of
\mathsf{G}
-bundles, i.e.,
a_{1}\in H^{1}(M,\mathsf{G})
.
Here
b_{n}(M)
is the
n
’th Betti number of
M
.
The theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
has a
(d-1)
form symmetry
{\mathsf{G}}^{\vee}={\rm{hom}}(\mathsf{G},\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathsf{G}

[
122
,
18
,
65
]
, denoted by
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}_{(d-1)}
.
The symmetry operator corresponding to an element
\mathsf{g}^{\vee}\in\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
defined on a 1-cycle
\gamma
is

W_{\mathsf{g}^{\vee}}(\gamma)=\exp\left\{{\rm i}g^{\vee}\oint_{\gamma}a_{1}\right\}\,.

(3.2)

We can couple
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
to a background
d
-form gauge field
A_{d}^{\vee}
, which
corresponds to inserting a network of line-like symmetry operators.
The partition function of
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
in the presence of a
A^{\vee}_{d}
is given by

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}[A^{\vee}_{d}]=\frac{1}{|\mathsf{G}|^{b_{0}(M)}}\sum_{a_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}[a_{1}]\exp\left\{{\rm i}\int_{M}a_{1}\cup A^{\vee}_{d}\right\}\,.

(3.3)

Gauge invariance of the partition function under background gauge transformations of
A^{\vee}_{d}
is guaranteed by the fact that
{\rm d}a_{1}=0
.
The
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}_{(d-1)}
global symmetry of the gauged theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
can itself be gauged to deliver a theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee\vee}
which again has a symmetry
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee\vee}}[A_{1}]=\frac{1}{|\mathsf{G}^{\vee}|^{\mathsf{N}_{d}(M)}}\sum_{a_{d}^{\vee}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}[a_{d}^{\vee}]\exp\left\{{\rm i}\int_{M}a_{d}^{\vee}\cup A_{1}\right\}\,,

(3.4)

where
\mathsf{N}_{p}(M)=\sum_{j=1}^{p}{(-1)}^{j+1}b_{p-j}(M)
.
Note that
N_{1}=b_{0}(M)
, which recovers (
3.3
).
Then inserting (
3.3
) into (
3.4
), one obtains

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee\vee}}[A_{1}]=\exp\left\{-\chi(M)\ln(|\mathsf{G}|)\right\}\,\times\,\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}[(-1)^{d}A_{1}]\,,

(3.5)

where
\chi(M)=\sum_{j=0}^{d+1}{(-1)}^{j}b_{j}(M)
is the Euler characteristic of the manifold
M
.
Here we used the relation

\delta(A_{d+1-n})=\frac{1}{|\mathsf{G}|^{b_{n}(M)}}\sum_{a_{n}\in H^{n}(M,\mathsf{G})}\exp\left\{\mathrm{i}\int_{M}a_{n}\cup A_{d+1-n}\right\}

(3.6)

Hence gauging twice acts as “charge conjugation" in odd spatial dimensions
[
123
]
upto a local curvature counter term.
In fact, the so-called Euler counterterm in (
3.5
) can be absorbed by redefining the normalization as
\mathsf{N}_{p}=\sqrt{|\mathsf{G}|^{b_{p}(M)}}
in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
).
We will however work with our initial choice of normalization as doing so simplifies the mapping of symmetry sectors between the original
\mathfrak{T}
and the gauged theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
.

3.1.2
Mapping of symmetry sectors

Using (
3.3
) and (
3.4
), one can see how the symmetry sectors on the different sides of the gauging-related duality map into each other.
Let us consider a
d+1
dimensional spacetime manifold that decomposes as
M=S^{1}\times M_{d}
, where
M_{d}
is the spatial
d
-manifold and
S^{1}
is the circle in the time direction, such as to connect with the Hamiltonian description in the rest of the paper.
We work with imaginary time and will therefore be considering thermal partition functions.
A
\mathsf{G}
background gauge field
A_{1}
(upto gauge transformations) is valued in
H^{1}(M,\mathsf{G})
, i.e., it is labelled by the holonomies of the gauge field
A_{1}
along the homology cycles of
M
.
Using the Künneth theorem, the 1st homology group of
M=S^{1}\times M_{d}
decomposes as

H_{1}(S^{1}\times M_{d},\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus H_{1}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z})={\rm Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\langle\widetilde{\gamma}\,,\gamma_{1}\,,\gamma_{2}\,,\dots\,,\gamma_{b_{1}(M_{d})}\rangle\,.

(3.7)

Then the gauge field
A_{1}
can be labelled by its holonomies around the homology cycles of
M
as
A_{1}=(\mathsf{g}_{t},\vec{\mathsf{g}})
where
\vec{\mathsf{g}}\equiv(\mathsf{g}_{1}\,,\mathsf{g}_{2}\,,\dots\,,\mathsf{g}_{b_{1}(M_{d})})\in H^{1}(M_{d},\mathsf{G})
and
\mathsf{g}_{t}\in\mathsf{G}
, i.e.,

\oint_{\gamma_{j}}A_{1}=\mathsf{g}_{j}\,,\qquad\oint_{\widetilde{\gamma}}A_{1}=\mathsf{g}_{t}\,.

(3.8)

The thermal partition function of a quantum system coupled to such a 0-form background gauge field is

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}[A_{1}]\equiv\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}[\mathsf{g}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{g}}]={\rm Tr}\left[\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{g}_{t}}\,e^{-\beta H_{\vec{\mathsf{g}}}}\right]\,,

(3.9)

where
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{g}_{t}}
is the symmetry operator corresponding to
\mathsf{g}_{t}
and
{H}_{\vec{\mathsf{g}}}
is the Hamiltonian of interest with
\mathsf{g}_{j}
twisted boundary conditions along the
j^{\rm th}
homology cycle of
M_{d}
.
In Sec.
2
, we could study the
\mathsf{G}
symmetric bond-algebra in a definite symmetry sector labelled by
\alpha\in\rm{Rep}(\mathsf{G})
and
\vec{\mathsf{g}}\in H^{1}(M_{d},\mathsf{G})
(see (
2.18
)).
Physically the label
\alpha
denoted the symmetry eigenspace we were restricting to and
\vec{\mathsf{g}}
was a choice of symmetry twisted boundary conditions.
In the spacetime partition function, this amounts to inserting a (codimension-1) projection operator
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}
at a fixed time, extending over all of
M_{d}
, which has the form

\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{|\mathsf{G}|}\sum_{\mathsf{g}_{t}\in\mathsf{G}}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\mathsf{g}_{t})^{-1}\,\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{g}_{t}}\,,

(3.10)

where
\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}:\mathsf{G}\to\mathsf{U}(1)
is the representation corresponding to the label
\alpha
.
We define a symmetry character
\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}[\alpha,\vec{\mathsf{g}}]
as the thermal trace in the sector transforming in the
\alpha
representation and with
\vec{\mathsf{g}}
twisted boundary conditions

\begin{split}\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}[\alpha,\vec{\mathsf{g}}]&={\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{V}_{M_{d}}}\left[\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}\,e^{-\beta H_{\vec{\mathsf{g}}}}\right]\,.\end{split}

(3.11)

After gauging, we obtain a dual theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
which can be coupled to a
d
-form gauge field
A_{d}\in H^{d}(M,\mathsf{G})={\rm Hom}(H_{d}(M\,,\mathbb{Z})\,,\mathsf{G})
.

Figure 10
:
The figure illustrates a spacetime
M=S^{1}\times T^{2}=T^{3}
where all space and time directions are periodic. The left figure depicts the insertion of
0
-form symmetry operators (surfaces) in theory
\mathfrak{T}
. The red and blue surfaces along
x
-
t
and
y
-
t
planes correspond to twisting the boundary conditions along the
x
and
y
direction. The green surface on a time slice is the projection operator (
3.10
), projecting to the symmetry sector
\mathcal{U}=\omega^{\alpha}
. The insertion of these in the partition function gives rise to the character
\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}[\alpha,\vec{\mathsf{g}}]
(see (
3.11
)). This contains the energy spectrum of the theory with twisted boundary conditions
\vec{\mathsf{g}}
and in symmetry sector
\alpha
.
After gauging the
0
-form
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetry we obtain the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
and the relation (
3.17
) between the characters of the two theories. Here
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
has a
(d-1)
-form symmetry (lines). The right-hand side illustrates the dual characters after gauging. The characters are equal, but the labels for twisted boundary conditions and symmetry sector has swapped.

Again, using the Künneth theorem,

\begin{split}H_{d}(S^{1}\times M_{d}\,,\mathbb{Z})&=H_{d-1}(M_{d}\,,\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{d}(M_{d}\,,\mathbb{Z})\\
&\cong H_{1}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\,.\end{split}

(3.12)

In the second line we have used the assumption that
M_{d}
is closed and oriented which via the Universal coefficient theorem and Poincaré duality implies
H_{d-1}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z})=H_{1}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z})
and the fact that
M_{d}
is path connected, which implies
H_{d}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
More precisely we can canonically identify the generators
\Sigma^{(d-1)}_{j}\in H_{d-1}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z})
with the generators
\gamma_{j}\in H_{1}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z})
.
The
d
-form background gauge field
A_{d}
can be labelled by its holonomies around the
d
-homology cycles of
M
as
A_{d}=(\vec{\mathsf{g}}^{\vee},\mathsf{g}_{t}^{\vee})
such that
\mathsf{g}_{t}^{\vee}\in\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
and
\vec{\mathsf{g}}^{\vee}=(\mathsf{g}^{\vee}_{1}\,,\dots\,,\mathsf{g}^{\vee}_{b_{1}(M_{d})})
such that

\oint_{M_{d}}A_{d}=\mathsf{g}_{t}^{\vee}\,,\qquad\oint_{\Sigma^{(d-1)}_{j}\times S^{1}}A_{d}=\mathsf{g}^{\vee}_{j}

(3.13)

The thermal partition function of the quantum system
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
coupled to
A_{d}
background has the form

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}[A_{d}^{\vee}]={\rm{Tr}}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{b_{1}(M_{d})}W_{\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\vee}}(\gamma_{j})\,e^{-\beta H^{\vee}_{\mathsf{g}_{t}^{\vee}}}\right]\,,

(3.14)

where
W_{\mathsf{g}^{\vee}_{j}}(\gamma_{j})
is the
(d-1)
-form symmetry operator (
3.2
) defined on the homology 1-cycle
\gamma_{j}
and
H^{\vee}
is a Hamiltonian of interest with
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}_{(d-1)}
-form global symmetry and
\mathsf{g}^{\vee}_{t}
twisted boundary conditions.
As before, we can project onto definite
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
eigenspaces of each of the line operators by using the projection operator

\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{g}}(\gamma_{j})=\frac{1}{|\mathsf{G}|}\sum_{\mathsf{g}^{\vee}_{j}\in\mathsf{G}^{\vee}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\vee}}(\mathsf{g})^{-1}\,W_{\mathsf{g}_{j}^{\vee}}(\gamma_{j})\,.

(3.15)

We define the symmetry character as a thermal trace in a definite symmetry sector with
\mathsf{g}_{t}^{\vee}\in\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
twisted boundary conditions and
\mathsf{g}_{j}
eigenvalues of the symmetry operator on the 1-cycle
\gamma_{j}

\chi_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}[\vec{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{g}_{t}^{\vee}]={\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{M_{d}}}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{b_{1}(M_{d})}\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{g}_{j}}(\gamma_{j})\,q^{-\beta H^{\vee}_{\mathsf{g}_{t}^{\vee}}}\right]\,.

(3.16)

Using (
3.3
), (
3.4
), (
3.11
) and (
3.16
) it can be shown that

\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}[\alpha,\vec{\mathsf{g}}]=\chi_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}[\vec{\mathsf{g}},\alpha]\,.

(3.17)

implying a duality between the corresponding sectors of the theory
\mathfrak{T}
with
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}
and theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
with global symmetry
\mathsf{G}_{(d-1)}^{\vee}
. See figure
10
for more details.

3.2
Gauging finite Abelian sub-symmetry

3.2.1
Dual symmetries and mixed anomaly

We now describe the more interesting case, where the full symmetry group
\mathsf{G}
of
\mathfrak{T}
, is a central extension of
\mathsf{K}
by
\mathsf{N}

[
18
,
65
,
45
]
.
More precisely,
\mathsf{G}
sits in the short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mathsf{N}\longrightarrow\mathsf{G}\longrightarrow\mathsf{K}\longrightarrow 1\,,

(3.18)

whose extension class is
\epsilon\in H^{2}(\mathsf{K},\mathsf{N})
.
This implies that a
\mathsf{G}
background gauge field
A_{1}
can be represented as a tuple
\left(A_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})},A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right)\in C^{1}(M,\mathsf{N})\times C^{1}(M,\mathsf{K})
which satisfy the modified cocyle conditions
[
65
]

{\rm{d}}A_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})}=\epsilon\left(A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right)\,,\qquad{\rm d}A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}=0\,.

(3.19)

Correspondingly, the partition function of
\mathfrak{T}
coupled to a
{\mathsf{G}}
gauge field is denoted by
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[A_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})},A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right]
.
Recall the notation
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{N}\times_{\epsilon}\mathsf{K}
from section
2.2
.
Instead of gauging the full group
\mathsf{G}
, consider gauging
\mathsf{N}\subset\mathsf{G}
, using Eq. (
3.3
) to obtain the partially gauged theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
.
The gauged theory has a residual 0-form symmetry
(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{N})_{(0)}\cong\mathsf{K}_{(\mathsf{0})}
. Additionally, there is also a dual
(d-1)
-form symmetry
\mathsf{N}^{\vee}_{(d-1)}
.
Hence after the partial gauging, the resulting symmetry is a higher
d
-group
\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}^{\epsilon}

\mathsf{G}_{(0)}=\mathsf{N}_{(0)}\times_{\epsilon}\mathsf{K}_{(0)}\xrightarrow{\;\text{gauging $\mathsf{N}_{(0)}$}\;}\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}^{\epsilon}=\left[\mathsf{K}_{(0)},\;\mathsf{N}_{(d-1)}^{\vee}\right]^{\epsilon}.

(3.20)

The partition function of the gauged theory can be coupled to background gauge fields of
\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}^{\epsilon}=\left[\mathsf{K}_{(0)},\;\mathsf{N}_{(d-1)}^{\vee}\right]^{\epsilon}
as

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}\left[A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\,,A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right]=\frac{1}{|\mathsf{H}|}\sum_{a_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[a_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})}\,,A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right]\exp\left\{{\rm i}\int_{M}a_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})}\cup A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\right\}\,.

(3.21)

Interestingly, the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
has a mixed ’t Hooft anomaly between
\mathsf{K}_{(0)}
and
\mathsf{N}^{\vee}_{(d-1)}
which manifests in the lack of invariance of the partition function under background gauge transformations

A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\longmapsto A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}+{\rm d}\lambda_{d-1}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\,,

(3.22)

where
\lambda_{d-1}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\in C^{d-1}(M,\mathsf{N}^{\vee})
is a gauge transformation parameter.
Under such a background gauge transformation, the partition function (
3.21
) transforms as

\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}\left[A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}+{\rm d}\lambda_{d-1}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\,,A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right]}{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}\left[A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})},A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right]}=\exp\left\{{\rm i}\int_{M}\lambda_{d-1}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\cup\epsilon\left(A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right)\right\}\,.

(3.23)

The lack of gauge invariance cannot be remedied by any choice of local counter-terms, however it can be absorbed by coupling
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
to an invertible topological field theory
[
74
,
75
]
known as the anomaly theory with the action

S_{\rm{anom}}=\int_{M_{d+2}}A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\cup\epsilon\left(A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right)\,,

(3.24)

Such an invertible field theory describes the ground state physics of a symmetry protected topological phase of matter protected by
\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}^{\epsilon}=\left[\mathsf{K}_{(0)},\;\mathsf{N}_{(d-1)}^{\vee}\right]^{\epsilon}
.
It is however crucial to emphasize that there is no physical need to associate
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
to a ‘bulk’.
As described in Sec.
2
, such a theory can very well be described on an
d
-dimensional lattice model.
Instead the bulk or anomaly theory is a theoretical gadget to systematize our understanding of the anomaly, which has significant non-perturbative implications for the infra-red phases/ground states realized in
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
.
Specifically, as we will demonstrate, a systems with such anomalies cannot have a gapped and symmetric (disordered) ground state.
Instead, any gapped ground state must break the symmetry down to a subgroup that trivializes the anomaly.
This is a consequence of anomaly matching between the ultraviolet and infra-red physics.

For the remainder of this section, we specialize to the case
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
and
\mathsf{N}_{(0)}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, in which case the symmetry of
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
is
\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}^{\epsilon}=\left[\mathbb{Z}_{2,(0)},\;\mathbb{Z}_{2,(d-1)}\right]^{\epsilon}
with the anomaly action having the explicit form

S_{\rm{anom}}={\rm i}\pi\int_{M_{d+2}}A_{d}\cup{\rm Bock}(A_{1})\,,

(3.25)

where
A_{p}\in H^{p}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
{\rm{Bock}}
denotes the Bockstein homomorphism (see Appendix B of
[
3
]
for details) which is a map of cohomology classes

\begin{split}{\rm{Bock}}&:H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})\longrightarrow H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{2})\,,\qquad{\rm{Bock}}(A_{1})=\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}\widetilde{A}_{1}\,,\end{split}

(3.26)

where
\widetilde{A}_{1}
is the lift of
A_{1}
to a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
gauge field.

3.2.2
Mapping of symmetry sectors

Under gauging of a subgroup, the symmetry sectors of the pre-gauged and gauged theories
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
respectively, map into each other in a non-trivial way.
In this section, we detail the map of sectors for the case of
\mathsf{N}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
.
However the analysis readily generalizes to any finite Abelian group
\mathsf{G}
with subgroup
\mathsf{N}
.
Since we want to gauge
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, it will be convenient to write a group element
\mathsf{g}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}\cong\left\{0,1,2,3\right\}
as a tuple
(\mathsf{n},\mathsf{k}
) such that
\mathsf{n},\mathsf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\left\{0\,,1\right\}
, with the identification
\mathsf{g}=2\mathsf{n}+\mathsf{k}
and the product rule in
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
given by

(\mathsf{n}_{1}\,,\mathsf{k}_{1})\cdot(\mathsf{n}_{2}\,,\mathsf{k}_{2})=(\mathsf{n}_{1}+\mathsf{n}_{2}+\mathsf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{k}_{2}\,,\mathsf{k}_{1}+\mathsf{k}_{2})\,.

(3.27)

A
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
gauge field
A_{1}^{(\mathsf{G})}\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{4})
for
M=M_{d}\times S^{1}
is labelled by its holonomies on the homology 1-cycles of
M
and can correspondingly be expressed as a tuple of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge fields
\left(A_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})}\,A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right)
with a modified cocycle condition (
3.19
) as

\begin{split}A_{1}^{(\mathsf{G})}&=(\mathsf{g}_{t},\mathsf{g}_{1},\cdots\mathsf{g}_{N})=(\mathsf{g}_{t},\vec{\mathsf{g}})\,,\\
A_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})}&=(\mathsf{n}_{t},\mathsf{n}_{1},\cdots\mathsf{n}_{N})=(\mathsf{n}_{t},\vec{\mathsf{n}})\,,\\
A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}&=(\mathsf{k}_{t},\mathsf{k}_{1},\cdots\mathsf{k}_{N})=(\mathsf{k}_{t},\vec{\mathsf{k}})\,,\end{split}

(3.28)

where
N=b_{1}(M_{d})
,
\vec{\mathsf{g}}\in H^{1}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z}_{4})
and
\vec{\mathsf{n}},\vec{\mathsf{k}}\in H^{1}(M_{d},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
The thermal partition function of
\mathfrak{T}
coupled to the background
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
gauge field
A_{1}^{(\mathsf{G})}
has the form

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[A_{1}^{(\mathsf{G})}\right]\equiv\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[\mathsf{g}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{g}}\right]\equiv\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[\mathsf{n}_{t}\,,\mathsf{k}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{n}}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right]={\rm{Tr}}\left[\mathcal{U}^{2n_{\mathsf{n}_{t}+\mathsf{k}_{t}}}\,e^{-\beta H_{\vec{\mathsf{n}},\vec{\mathsf{k}}}}\right]\,,

(3.29)

where
\mathcal{U}
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
generator and
H_{\vec{\mathsf{n}},\vec{\mathsf{k}}}
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetric Hamiltonian with
\vec{\mathsf{g}}=(\vec{\mathsf{n}},\vec{\mathsf{k}})
twisted boundary conditions.
With the purpose, of tracking how symmetry sectors map under partial-gauging, we define
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{\mathsf{g}}}
which is a projector onto the sub Hilbert space transforming in the
\alpha_{\mathsf{g}}
representation of
\rm{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})

\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{\mathsf{g}}}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\mathsf{g}_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}}{\rm i}^{-\alpha_{\mathsf{g}}\mathsf{g}_{t}}\times\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{g}_{t}}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\mathsf{n}_{t},\mathsf{k}_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(-1)^{\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{n}_{t}+\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\mathsf{k}_{t}}{\rm i}^{-\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{k}_{t}}\times\mathcal{U}^{2\mathsf{n}_{t}+\mathsf{k}_{t}}\,,

(3.30)

where we have used
\alpha_{\mathsf{g}}=2\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}+\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}
.
Using (
3.30
) we may define a symmetry character
\chi[q,\alpha_{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{g}]
as the thermal trace in a definite eigensector
\alpha_{\mathsf{g}}\in\rm{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
and with symmetry twisted boundary conditions
\vec{\mathsf{g}}=2\vec{\mathsf{n}}+\vec{\mathsf{k}}
as

\begin{split}\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}[\alpha_{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{g}]&={\rm Tr}\left[\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{\mathsf{g}}}\,q^{-H_{\vec{\mathsf{n}},\vec{\mathsf{k}}}}\right]=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\mathsf{n}_{t},\mathsf{k}_{t}}(-1)^{\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{n}_{t}+\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\mathsf{k}_{t}}{\rm i}^{-\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{k}_{t}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[\mathsf{n}_{t}\,,\mathsf{k}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{n}}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right]\,.\end{split}

(3.31)

The expression (
3.31
) can be readily inverted to express the partition function in terms of the symmetry characters as

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[\mathsf{n}_{t}\,,\mathsf{k}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{n}}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right]=\sum_{\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}\,,\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}}(-1)^{\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{n}_{t}+\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\mathsf{k}_{t}}{\rm i}^{\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}\mathsf{k}_{t}}\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[2\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}+\alpha_{\mathsf{n}}\,,2\vec{\mathsf{n}}+\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right]\,.

(3.32)

Next, gauging the subgroup
\mathsf{N}\subset\mathsf{G}
simply corresponds to summing over the symmetry background
\mathsf{A}_{1}^{(\mathsf{N})}
.
The partition function of the partially gauged theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
has a
d
-group
\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}^{\epsilon}=\left[\mathsf{K}_{(0)},\mathsf{N}_{(d-1)}^{\vee}\right]^{\epsilon}
global symmetry and can therefore be coupled to a symmetry background
\left(A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\,,A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\right)
.
In particular, the symmetry background to
A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}
are labelled by
\vec{\mathsf{n}}^{\vee}
and
\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}
, see (
3.13
).
The partition function of
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
coupled to
(A_{1,\mathsf{K}},A_{d,\mathsf{N}^{\vee}})
is

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}\left[A_{d}^{(\mathsf{N}^{\vee})}\,,A_{1}^{(\mathsf{K})}\right]&\equiv\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}\left[\vec{\mathsf{n}}^{\vee}\,,\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}\,,\mathsf{k}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{n}_{t},\vec{\mathsf{n}}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}\left[\mathsf{n}_{t}\,,\mathsf{k}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{n}}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right](-1)^{\mathsf{n}_{t}\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}+\vec{\mathsf{n}}\cdot\vec{\mathsf{n}}^{\vee}}\,.\end{split}

(3.33)

As a thermal partition function, this may be expressed as

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}\left[\vec{\mathsf{n}}^{\vee}\,,\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}\,,\mathsf{k}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right]={\rm{Tr}}\left[\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{k}_{t}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}W^{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{j}}_{\gamma_{j}}\,e^{-\beta H^{\vee}_{\vec{\mathsf{k}}\,,\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}}}\right]\,,

(3.34)

where
\mathcal{U}
and
W_{\gamma_{j}}
are the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
0-form and
(d-1)
-form symmetry generators in the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
respectively.
Since we are interested in relating the symmetry-resolved energy spectra of the two theories
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
, we need to define the character of the dual theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
as well. However, due to the mixed anomaly in this theory, see equation (
2.78
) and figure
7
, some slight care is needed to define characters correctly. In particular, equation (
2.78
) implies that in twisted sectors the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry operators can square to
-1
instead of
+1
and thus have ’fractionalized’ symmetry eigenvalues
\pm\rm i
instead of
\pm 1
. The appropriate symmetry projector for the
\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}^{\epsilon}=\left[\mathsf{K}_{(0)},\mathsf{N}_{(d-1)}^{\vee}\right]^{\epsilon}
is thus
12
12

12

Note that technically only
\mathcal{P}^{(W)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(\mathcal{U})}
are projectors to symmetry eigenspaces (for the
(d-1)
and
0
-form symmetries, respectively). The ’twisted BC projectors’
\mathcal{P}^{(\mathcal{T}^{(d-1})}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(\mathcal{T}^{(0})}
are not real projectors, and somewhat trivial. But we include these here to easier keep track of how symmetry sectors and boundary conditions swap under gauging dualities.

\mathcal{P}[\vec{\alpha}_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}}\,,\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\,,\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}]=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\mathcal{P}^{(W)}_{\alpha_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{j}},\mathsf{k}_{j}}\,P^{(\mathcal{U})}_{\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\,,\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}}\,\mathcal{P}^{(\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)})}_{\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}}\,\mathcal{P}^{(\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma_{j}})}_{\mathsf{k}_{j}}\,,

(3.35)

where the superscript of each projector denotes the operator whose eigenspace is being projected onto while the subscripts denote the eigenvalues.
Explicitly, these projection operators have the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{(W)}_{\alpha_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{j}},\mathsf{k}_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+(-1)^{\alpha_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{j}}}{\rm i}^{-\mathsf{k}_{j}}W_{\gamma_{j}}}{2}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{(\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma_{j}})}_{\mathsf{k}_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+(-1)^{\mathsf{k}_{j}}\mathcal{T}_{\gamma_{j^{\prime}}}^{(0)}}{2}

(3.36)

\displaystyle P^{(\mathcal{U})}_{\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\,,\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+(-1)^{\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}}{\rm i}^{-\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}}\mathcal{U}}{2}\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{(\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)})}_{\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+(-1)^{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}}\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)}}{2}\,,

where
\mathcal{U}
,
W_{\gamma_{j}}
,
\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma_{j}}
and
\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)}
are defined in (
2.76
), (
2.77
) and (
2.78
).
Here the symmetry characters of the gauged theory are labelled by a representation
\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}
and
\vec{\alpha}_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}}=(\alpha_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{1}}\,,\dots\,,\alpha_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{N}})
, while twisted boundary conditions are labeled by
\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}
and
\vec{\mathsf{k}}
. Inserting this into the partition function, the characters of
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
take the form (see also
[
124
]
for a similar discussion in
1+1
dimensions)

\chi_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}\left[\vec{\alpha}_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}}\,,\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\,,\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right]=\frac{1}{2^{1+N}}\sum_{\mathsf{k}_{t},\vec{\mathsf{n}}^{\vee}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}\left[\vec{\mathsf{n}}^{\vee}\,,\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}\,,\mathsf{k}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}\right](-1)^{\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\mathsf{k}_{t}+\vec{\alpha}_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}}\cdot\vec{\mathsf{n}}^{\vee}}\,{\rm i}^{-\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}\mathsf{k}_{t}-\vec{\mathsf{k}}\cdot{\vec{\mathsf{n}}}^{\vee}}\,.

(3.37)

The appearance of
\rm i
in the characters above, is a consequence of symmetry fractionalization stemming from the mixed anomaly. The symmetry characters of
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
can be written in terms of the characters of
\mathfrak{T}
by using (
3.33
) and (
3.32
).
However, let us instead derive this relation using the lattice realization of the symmetry structures of
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
described in Sec.
2
.
To extract the mapping of sectors, we note that a sector in the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
labelled as
(\vec{\alpha}_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}}\,,\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\,,\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}})
is the sub-Hilbert space of
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{M_{d}}
in the simultaneous image of the projection operators (
3.35
).
The gauging map in Sec.
2.2
relates
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(0)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2,(d-1)}
symmetric operators on
\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{M_{d}}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetric operators on
\mathcal{V}_{M_{d}}
.
In particular there is the following mapping of operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}^{\vee}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{U}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}}=\prod_{\mathsf{v}}X_{\mathsf{v}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(d-1)}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}^{\vee}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{U}^{2}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}}\,,

(3.38)

\displaystyle W_{\gamma}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}^{\vee}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{T}^{(0)}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}}=\prod_{\mathsf{e}\subset\gamma}Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{\phantom{\dagger}}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{\dagger}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{\gamma_{j}}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}^{\vee}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left[\mathcal{T}^{(0)}\right]^{2}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}}\,.

Using these operator isomorphisms, the product of projectors in (
3.35
) in the theory
\mathfrak{T}
maps to a product of projectors in the theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
.
More precisely, one obtains a projector onto the
2\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}-\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}\in\rm{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{4})
eigensector of
\mathcal{U}
and the sector with
2\alpha_{n^{\vee}_{j}}-\mathsf{k}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry twisted boundary conditions along the cycle
\gamma_{j}
.
Which implies the following map of symmetry sectors

\chi_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}[q,\vec{\alpha}_{\mathsf{n}^{\vee}}\,,\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}\,,\mathsf{n}^{\vee}_{t}\,,\vec{\mathsf{k}}]=\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}[q\,,2\alpha_{\mathsf{k}}+\mathsf{n}_{t}^{\vee}\,,2\vec{\alpha}_{n^{\vee}}+\vec{\mathsf{k}}]\,.

(3.39)

4
Phase diagrams and dualities in
d=2

Having detailed the gauging of finite Abelian symmetries and their subgroups both in the lattice setting in Sec.
2
and more generally in a space-time approach in Sec.
3
, we now turn our attention to the action of gauging-related dualities on phase diagrams in two-dimensional space.
More precisely, let us again consider a theory
\mathfrak{T}
with global symmetry
\mathsf{G}
by which we mean a parameter space of
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric Hamiltonians.
This space of local Hamiltonians is contained within the bond algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Upon gauging either the full group
\mathsf{G}
or a certain subgroup, we obtain a new theory
\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}
, i.e., a parameter space of models in the bond algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{G}^{\vee}}(\mathcal{V}^{\vee})
, where
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
is typically a higher group, potentially with mixed anomalies.
Since the gauging map is an isomorphism between the bond algebras, the physics before and after gauging is intimately related, or more precisely dual.
This duality is evident in several aspects.
For instance, the spectrum of a
\mathsf{G}
symmetric Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
and its
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
-symmetric image
\mathcal{H}^{\vee}
under (partial) gauging have the same spectrum in ‘dual’ symmetry sectors, in the sense detailed in Sec.
3
.
Another consequence is the equality of correlation functions

\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{x}_{1}\,,\mathsf{t}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n}(\mathsf{x}_{n}\,,\mathsf{t}_{n})\rangle_{\Phi}=\langle\mathcal{O}^{\vee}_{1}(\mathsf{x}_{1}\,,\mathsf{t}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}^{\vee}_{n}(\mathsf{x}_{n}\,,\mathsf{t}_{n})\rangle_{\Phi^{\vee}}\,,

(4.1)

where
\Phi
collectively denotes the symmetry sector labels of theory
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathcal{O}_{j}
are operators in the bond algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{G}}
.

\Phi^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\vee}_{j}
are the images of
\Phi
and
\mathcal{O}_{j}
under the gauging map.

4.1
Gauging finite Abelian symmetry

In this section, we describe how the phase diagrams of a theory with 0-form and 1-form
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetry are related.
Our analysis generalizes to any finite Abelian group
\mathsf{G}
with a few caveats which we will elucidate as we go along.
To organize the mapping of phase diagrams under (partial) gauging, we seek to enumerate

\mathsf{G}
-symmetric gapped phases in two dimensions.
Doing so, one immediately encounters a complication in the fact that there are infinitely many
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric gapped phases of matter if one includes phases with long range entanglement.
This should not come as a surprise as the set of gapped phases (which admit a lattice Hamiltonian description) without any symmetry enrichment is already infinitely large and contains all topological orders with gapped boundaries.
This set is at least as rich as fusion categories since a topological order (with a gappable boundary) can be constructed from a given fusion category as input
[
125
,
126
,
113
]
.
Here, we content ourselves by investigating the parameter space of
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}
symmetric short range entangled (SRE) systems.

SRE gapped phases are classified by tuples
[\mathsf{H},\nu]
where
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathsf{G}
and
\nu\in H^{3}(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U}(1))
.
A phase thus labelled spontaneously breaks the global symmetry down to
\mathsf{H}
.
Therefore, such a phase possesses
|\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{H}|
ground states that form an orbit under the action of
\mathsf{G}
.
Furthermore, each such ground state is a symmetry protected topological (SPT) phase labelled by
\nu\in H^{3}(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U}(1))
of the unbroken
\mathsf{H}
subgroup
[
127
]
.
We consider the following
\mathsf{G}
-symmetric Hamiltonians which can access all SRE gapped phases

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}[\lambda]=\sum_{\mathsf{H},\,\nu}\lambda_{[\mathsf{H},\nu]}\,\mathcal{H}^{\,}_{[\mathsf{H},\nu]},

(4.2)

where the parameters
\lambda_{[\mathsf{H},\nu]}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\,}_{[\mathsf{H},\nu]}
is a fixed-point Hamiltonian for each gapped phase.
Setting all
\lambda_{[\mathsf{H},\nu]}=0
except for one pair
[\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}]

selects a fixed-point Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}^{\,}_{[\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}]}
realizing the gapped phase labelled by
[\mathsf{H}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}]
.

Upon gauging
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}
, one realizes a model with a dual
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}_{(1)}
global symmetry.
Correspondingly, each
\mathsf{G}_{(0)}
symmetric gapped phase
[\mathsf{H},\nu]
maps to a
\mathsf{G}_{(1)}^{\vee}
symmetric gapped phase we label
[\mathsf{H},\nu]^{\vee}
.
A natural question then is where does
[\mathsf{H},\nu]^{\vee}
fit into the classification of
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}_{(1)}
symmetric gapped phases?
We will see that the data
[\mathsf{H},\nu]
correspond to information pertaining to (i) the 1-form symmetry that is preserved in the dual model and (ii) the topological properties of certain emergent 1-form symmetries that arise in phases with spontaneously broken 1-form symmetry.
To dissect these statements let us first describe what is meant by 1-form symmetry breaking?

1-form symmetry breaking:

Similar to conventional 0-form symmetry breaking, 1-form symmetry breaking is also signalled by long-range order of an operator which is charged under the relevant symmetry.
In the case of 0-form symmetry, the charged local operator is the local order parameter.
In contrast, 1-form symmetry breaking is diagnosed by a perimeter law expectation value of a closed line operator which is charged under the 1-form symmetry in question
[
128
,
67
,
56
,
129
]
.
In the parlance of gauge theory, the charged line operator is deconfined in the 1-form symmetry broken phase.
In the infra-red/low-energy limit, such a charged line operator becomes topological, i.e., its expectation value is invariant under topological deformations.
Furthermore, tautologically, there is a non-trivial linking between the charged line and 1-form symmetry generator (see fig
11
).
Therefore the low energy theory contains topological line operators that braid non-trivially and is topologically ordered
[
10
]
.
These phases are paradigmatic examples of models with long-range entanglement and therefore one observes that gauging maps short range entangled phases to long range entangled phases in
2+1
dimensions
[
69
,
130
,
71
]

\left\langle\phantom{{{{A^{B}}^{C}}^{X}}^{X}}\cdots\right\rangle
=
\left\langle\phantom{{{{A^{B}}^{C}}^{X}}^{X}}\cdots\right\rangle
\hobbyinit
\hobby@initial@pt
\hobbysetparams
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbyaddpoint
\hobbysetparams
\hobbygenusepath
\left\langle\phantom{{{{A^{B}}^{C}}^{X}}^{X}}\cdots\right\rangle
\alpha
\mathsf{g}
=
\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\mathsf{g})\left\langle\phantom{{{{A^{B}}^{C}}^{X}}^{X}}\cdots\right\rangle
\alpha

Figure 11
:
1-form symmetry breaking is signalled by the deconfinement of a line operator which is charged under the 1-form symmetry generator.
In the infra-red/low energy limit, such an operator becomes topological.

Let us consider the case where
\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
The gapped phases are labelled by the pair
[\mathbb{Z}_{p}cmcm\ell]
with
p
a divisor of
n
and
\ell\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathsf{U}(1))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
Before analyzing the dualities on the lattice, let us first get some intuition about how the gapped phases are mapped under gauging.
To do so we turn to topological partition functions, which
encode the low energy physics within a given gapped phase.
For
\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, the topological partition function for the gapped phase labelled by
[\mathbb{Z}_{p},\ell]
has the form

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}}[M\,,A_{1}]=\frac{n}{p}\delta_{pA_{1},0}\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\ell}{p}\int_{M}A_{1}\cup{\rm Bock}(A_{1})\right\},

(4.3)

where the Bockstein homomorphism
{\rm Bock}:H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{p})\to H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{p})
is implemented by

{\rm Bock}(A_{1})=\frac{1}{p}\,\rm{d}\widetilde{A}^{\,}_{1},

(4.4)

and
\widetilde{A}^{\,}_{1}
is a lift of
A^{\,}_{1}
to
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{p^{2}}
.
The factor of
\delta_{pA_{1},0}
implies that the theory cannot be coupled to a
\mathbb{Z}_{n/p}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p}
as it is broken in this phase.
Meanwhile the topological action in the exponent of (
4.3
) is the SPT response theory/effective action of the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
global symmetry which remains unbroken.
Then the low-energy or ground state physics of the dual gapped phase obtained after gauging, can be extracted using (
3.3
).
The topological partition function corresponding to this dual gapped phase has the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}[M]&=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{a_{1}\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{p})}\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\ell}{p}\int_{M}a_{1}\cup{\rm Bock}(a_{1})\right\}\,,\\
&=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{a_{1},{b}_{1}\in C^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{p})}\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{p}\int_{M}\left[{b}_{1}\cup{\rm d}a_{1}+{\ell}a_{1}\cup\frac{\rm{d}{a}_{1}}{p}\right]\right\}\,.\end{split}

(4.5)

This theory is a
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
Dijkgraaf-Witten theory
[
131
]
with the topological action labelled with
\ell\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathsf{U}(1))

[
132
,
130
]
.
In the second line, we have simply re-formulated the Dijkgraaf-Witten theory in terms of 1-cochains
a_{1}\,,b_{1}\in C^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, which is the quantum double formulation (see
[
130
]
and references therein).
The additional field
b_{1}
serves to parametrize/probe the aforementioned emergent 1-form symmetry and can be summed over to go back to the first line.
By inspecting the equations of motion, the most general topological operator can be read off to be

\begin{split}W_{(\rm{q,m})}(L)=\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{p}\oint_{L}({\rm q}\,a_{1}+{\rm m}\,b_{1})\right\}\,,\end{split}

(4.6)

where
{\rm q,m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
We note that
W_{(0,1)}(L)
generates an emergent 1-form symmetry.
Notice that due to the delta function
\delta_{pA_{1},0}
in (
4.3
), the following holds true

\left\langle\exp\left\{{\rm i}p\oint_{L}a_{1}\right\}\right\rangle=1\,,

(4.7)

for any 1-cycle
L
.
Since the operator
\exp\left\{{\rm i}p\oint_{L}a_{1}\right\}
generates a
\mathbb{Z}_{n/p,(1)}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n,(1)}
symmetry, (
4.7
) implies that all lines charged under
\mathbb{Z}_{n/p}
are confined and do not appear in the infra-red fixed point.
This is equivalent to the fact that
\mathbb{Z}_{n/p,(1)}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n,(1)}
remains unbroken.

Next let us inspect the fate of the remaining
\mathbb{Z}_{p,(1)}
generated by
W_{(1,0)}
.
To do so, we turn on sources for the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
1-form symmetry as well as the emergent 1-form symmetry.
This can be done by introducing two 2-form background gauge fields
A_{2}\,,B_{2}\in Z^{2}(M\,,\mathbb{Z}_{p})
that enter the partition function as

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\vee}}[M,A_{2},B_{2}]&=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{a_{1},b_{1}}\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{p}\int_{M}\left[b_{1}\cup{\rm d}a_{1}+{\ell}a_{1}\cup\frac{\rm{d}a_{1}}{p}+A_{2}\cup a_{1}+B_{2}\cup b_{1}\right]\right\}\,.\end{split}

(4.8)

The correlation functions of the topological line operators can be computed straightforwardly using standard methods
[
132
,
133
]
.
For instance consider two 1-cycles
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
embedded in
M=S^{3}
such that they form a Hopf-link.
The correlation function of two line operators with support on
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
is

\Big\langle W_{(\rm{q}_{1},\rm{m}_{1})}(L_{1})W_{(\rm{q}_{2},\rm{m}_{2})}(L_{2})\Big\rangle=\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{p}({\rm{q}}_{1}{\rm{m}}_{2}+{\rm{q}}_{2}{\rm{m}}_{1})+\frac{4\pi i\ell}{p^{2}}{\rm m_{1}m_{2}}\right\}\,.

(4.9)

Hence, the parameter
\ell\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathsf{U}(1))
changes the self-braiding and topological spin of the emergent 1-form symmetry generators.

4.1.1
SPT Hamiltonians on the lattice

We now turn to how these features manifest on the lattice.
As before we consider a triangulated manifold
M_{2,\triangle}
with each vertex endowed with a local Hilbert space
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
We investigate how fixed-point Hamiltonians dualize under gauging.
A fixed point Hamiltonian in the phase labelled as
[\mathbb{Z}_{p},\ell]
has the form

\mathcal{H}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{p},\ell]}=-\sum_{\mathsf{v}}X_{\mathsf{v}}^{n/p}\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\ell}{p^{2}}\sum_{\mathsf{e}\subset\partial\rm{Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\right\}-\sum_{\mathsf{e}}Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{p}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{-p}+\rm{H.c}\,,

(4.10)

where
\rm{Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}
denotes the smallest hexagon in the direct lattice
enclosing vertex
\mathsf{v}
and
\partial\rm{Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}
is the set of six edges at the boundary of this hexagon (see Fig.
12
).

\mathsf{v}^{\prime}
\mathsf{v}
\rm{Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}
\partial\rm{Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}
\mathsf{e}
\mathsf{e}^{\vee}
S^{1}_{\mathsf{v}}

\mathsf{v}
L
L^{\vee}

Figure 12
:
(a)
\rm{Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}
denotes the smallest hexagon (in red) on the direct lattice enclosing vertex
\mathsf{v}
and
\partial\rm{Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}
is the set of six edges (in green) on the boundary of this hexagon.
The triangular lattice is dual to the hexagonal lattice such that each edge
\mathsf{e}
of the triangular lattice is associated to an edge
\mathsf{e}^{\vee}
of the dual lattice.
We denote a unit cell of the dual lattice that links with the vertex
\mathsf{v}
of the direct lattice as
S^{1}_{\mathsf{v}}
.
(b) The Hamiltonian (
4.22
) is sum of framed ribbon operators linking each vertex
\mathsf{v}
of the triangular lattice.
The figure (b) also depicts a twisted ribbon operator.

\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{e}}
is defined as

\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{e}}=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{p-1}\alpha\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{e}}^{(\alpha)}\,,\qquad\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{e}}^{(\alpha)}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\tau=0}^{n-1}e^{-\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\tau\alpha}{p}}(Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{\phantom{\dagger}}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{\dagger})^{\tau}\,.

(4.11)

We are interested in the groundstate symmetry properties of (
4.10
).
Since
X_{\mathsf{v}}^{n/p}
and
Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{p}
commute, (
4.10
) is a commuting projector Hamiltonian and the ground states lie in
Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{p}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{-p}=1
subspace of the Hilbert space.
It follows that in this subspace
Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}^{\phantom{\dagger}}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{\dagger}
have eigenvalues that are the
p^{\rm th}
roots of unity and consequently
\mathcal{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\mathsf{e}}
is a projector onto the
\exp\left\{2\pi{\rm i}\alpha/p\right\}
eigenspace of this operator.
The Hilbert space splits in
n/p
super-selection sectors, that are dynamically disconnected in the thermodynamic (infinite size) limit as

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}&=\bigoplus_{\mathsf{g}=0}^{n/p-1}\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{g}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{g}}={\rm Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left\{|\phi\rangle_{\mathsf{g}}\,\big|\,\phi\in C^{0}(M_{\triangle},\mathbb{Z}_{p})\right\}\,,\end{split}

(4.12)

such that

Z_{\mathsf{v}}|\phi\rangle_{\mathsf{g}}=\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\phi_{\mathsf{v}}}{p}+\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\mathsf{g}}{n}\right\}|\phi\rangle_{\mathsf{g}}\,.

(4.13)

Note that the sub-Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{V}_{g}
are distinct eigenspaces of
Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{p}
, which is the order parameter of spontaneously breaking of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetry to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
Since the different super-selection sectors are dynamically disconnected, we may look at the Hamiltonian (
4.10
) in a specific subspace
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{g}}
, which we denote by

\mathcal{H}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{p},\ell]}^{(g)}=\mathcal{H}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{p},\ell]}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{g}}}\,.

(4.14)

Since,
Z_{\mathsf{v}}\,X_{\mathsf{v}}^{n/p}=\exp\left\{2\pi{\rm i}/p\right\}\,X^{n/p}_{\mathsf{v}}\,Z_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}
, the operators
\left\{X_{\mathsf{v}}^{n/p}\,,Z_{\mathsf{v}}\right\}
restricted to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{g}}
generate a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
clock and shift algebra, i.e.,

\begin{split}Z_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}Z_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}^{\dagger}|\phi\rangle_{\mathsf{g}}&=\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{p}({\rm d}\phi)_{\mathsf{e}}\right\}|\phi\rangle_{\mathsf{g}}\,,\\
X_{\mathsf{v}}|\phi\rangle_{\mathsf{g}}&=|\phi+\delta_{\mathsf{v}}\rangle_{\mathsf{g}}\,.\end{split}

(4.15)

We define effective
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
degrees of freedom in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{g}}
as

X_{\mathsf{v}}^{n/p}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{g}}}=e^{{\rm i}\Theta_{\mathsf{v}}}\,,\qquad Z_{\mathsf{v}}\Big|_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{g}}}=e^{{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{v}}}\,,

(4.16)

which satisfy the commutation relations
\left[\Theta_{\mathsf{v}}\,,\Phi_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}\right]=2\pi{\rm i}\delta_{\mathsf{v},\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}/{p}
.
In terms of these effective
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
degrees of freedom, we have

\mathcal{H}^{(\mathsf{g})}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{p},\ell]}=-\sum_{\mathsf{v}}e^{{\rm i}\Theta_{\mathsf{v}}}\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\ell}{p^{2}}\oint_{\partial{\rm Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}}{\rm d}\Phi\right\}\,.

(4.17)

This is nothing but a fixed point Hamiltonian for the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-SPT labelled by
\ell\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathsf{U}(1))

[
69
]
.
Therefore, we have demonstrated that the ground states of the Hamiltonian (
4.10
) break the global symmetry down to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n}
and furthermore, each symmetry broken ground state realizes an SPT of the unbroken symmetry.

:

Summary of dualities between

\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{4,(0)}
-symmetric SRE gapped phases
and
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{4,(1)}
-symmetric models with
gapped phases in
d=2
spatial dimensions.
The SRE ground states of
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{4,(0)}
-symmetric
Hamiltonians are described by the pair
[\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{p,(0)},\ell^{\,}_{p}]
.
Here, the subgroup
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{p,(0)}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{4,(0)}
with
p=1,2,4

denotes the symmetry preserved by the
4/p
-fold degenerate ground states.
We refer to the case
p=1
as spontaneously symmetry broken (SSB), while
the case of
p=2
is referred to as partial symmetry broken (PSB).
The index
\ell_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
labels the SPT phase supported by
each of the
4/p
-fold degenerate ground states, which we refer to as
{\rm SPT}(\ell_{p})
. The dual models are obtained by gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{4,(0)}
global symmetry. On the dual side, a phase preserving
\mathbb{Z}_{p,(0)}
symmetry
maps to a phase preserving
\mathbb{Z}_{4/p,(1)}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{4,(1)}
subgroup.
In this case, the ground state has an emergent
\mathbb{Z}_{p,(1)}
symmetry whose generator
carries topological spin
\theta=\pi\,\ell^{\,}_{p}
. We denote such dual ground states by

[\mathbb{Z}_{p,(1)},\,\ell^{\,}_{p}]
.
By
``\rm Triv.^{\prime\prime}
we mean the trivial symmetry group.

4.1.2
Dual Hamiltonian with higher-form symmetry

Now we turn to gauging the global
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
0-form symmetry.
The model after gauging is defined on
M_{2,\triangle}
with the degrees of freedom defined on the edges instead of the vertices.
The dual Hamiltonians can be obtained by using the bond algebra isomorphsim between (
2.16
) and (
2.36
).
The
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
0-form symmetric fixed point Hamiltonian (
4.10
) in the phase
[\mathbb{Z}_{p}\,,\ell]
maps to the following dual Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{p},\ell]}=-\sum_{\mathsf{v}}A^{n/p}_{\mathsf{v}}\exp\left\{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\ell}{p^{2}}\sum_{\mathsf{e}\subset\partial{\rm Hex}_{\mathsf{v}}}\mathcal{B}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{e}}\right\}-\sum_{\mathsf{e}}Z_{\mathsf{e}}^{p}+{\rm H.c},

(4.18)

where

{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}_{e}=\sum_{\alpha^{\vee}=0}^{p-1}\alpha^{\vee}\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{e}}^{(\alpha^{\vee})}\,,\qquad\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{e}}^{(\alpha^{\vee})}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\tau=0}^{n-1}e^{-\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\tau\alpha^{\vee}}{p}}\,Z^{\tau}_{\mathsf{e}}.

(4.19)

Since the different terms in the Hamiltonian commute, it immediately follows that the groundstate is in the
Z^{p}_{\mathsf{e}}=1
subspace of the Hilbert space.
As a straightforward consequence, we have that

\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in L}(Z_{\mathsf{e}}^{p})^{\mathsf{o}(\mathsf{e},L)}=1

(4.20)

for any closed loop
L
.
Since this line operator is the generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{n/p,(1)}
1-form symmetry, this implies that the ground state is invariant under this 1-form symmetry.
Let us now investigate the fate of the remaining
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
As before, we note that in a definite eigenspace of
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{e}}^{p}
, the operators
X_{\mathsf{e}}^{n/p}
and
Z_{\mathsf{e}}
generate a clock and shift algebra.
We use the representation

Z_{\mathsf{e}}\Bigg|_{Z^{p}_{\mathsf{e}}=1}=e^{{\rm i}a_{\mathsf{e}}}\,,\qquad X^{n/p}_{\mathsf{e}}\Bigg|_{Z^{p}_{\mathsf{e}}=1}=e^{{\rm i}b_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee}}}\,,

(4.21)

where we have used the convention that the
b^{\,}_{e^{\vee}}
operators
are defined on the links of the dual lattice.
They satisfy the algebra

\left[b_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee}}\,,a_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}\right]={2\pi{\rm i}\,{\rm Int}_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee},\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}}/{p}
, where
{\rm Int}_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee},\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}
denotes the intersection number of edge
\mathsf{e}^{\vee}
and
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
.
Using this representation, we may express the Hamiltonian (
4.18
) in the restricted
Z_{\mathsf{e}}^{p}=1
subspace as

\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{p},\ell]}\Bigg|_{Z_{\mathsf{e}}=1}=-\sum_{v}\exp\left\{{\rm i}\oint_{S^{1}_{\mathsf{v}}}b+\frac{{\rm i}\ell}{p}\oint_{\partial{\rm Hex_{\mathsf{v}}}}a\right\}\,.

(4.22)

This is nothing but the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
twisted quantum double Hamiltonian.
The Hamiltonian is a sum over framed ribbon operators linking with the vertices of the direct lattice (see Fig.
12
).
More generally, the line

W(\Gamma)=\exp\left\{{\rm i}\oint_{L^{\vee}}b+\frac{{\rm i}\ell}{p}\oint_{L}a\right\}\,,

(4.23)

commutes with the Hamiltonian and is therefore topological, i.e., an emergent 1 form symmetry generator.
Note that on the lattice one needs to provide two curves–
L
and
L^{\vee}
since
W(\Gamma)
is a framed line operator.
In terms of the Wilson operators defined in (
4.6
), this topological line operator is
W_{0,1}(\Gamma)
with
\Gamma=(L,L^{\vee})
.
Table
1
summarizes the dualities
between Hamiltonians (
4.10
) and
(
4.22
), when
n=4
and
p=1,2,4
.

The discussion in this section generalizes to any finite Abelian group
\mathsf{G}
.
Similar to the case of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, the gapped phases are labelled
[\mathsf{H},\nu]
, labelling the symmetry
\mathsf{H}
that is preserved by the ground state and the SPT class
\nu\in H^{3}(\mathsf{H}\,,\mathsf{U}(1))
of each symmetry broken ground state.
There are three possible types of cocycles for any finite Abelian group which are denoted as type-I, type-II and type-III cocycles
[
134
]
.
The physics of type-I and type-II cocycles is a straightforward generalization of the case of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
presented in this section, however the physics of type-III is different.
In particular after gauging an SPT labelled by a type-III cocycle
\nu
, one obtains a topological order with emergent non-invertible symmetries
[
134
,
135
,
130
]
.

4.2
Gauging finite Abelian sub-symmetry

In this section, we study the dualities between two theories related by the partial gauging of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{4}
0-form symmetry.
Concretely, we gauge the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup of a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetric system that can access all SRE gapped phases, i.e., combinations of SPT and symmetry broken phases.
Such phases are labelled by
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{4}
and a 3-cocycle (SPT action)
\nu\in H^{3}(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U}(1))
.
There are a total of seven such gapped phases since there are three subgroups (
\mathbb{Z}_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{1}
) of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
and
H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{n},\mathsf{U}(1))=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
We follow two complimentary strategies (i) partially gauge the topological (fixed-point) partition functions of each of the gapped phases in the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
model as in Sec.
3.2
and (ii) carry out a partial gauging of a specific lattice spin model using the bond algebra isomorphism described in Sec.
2.2
.
We summarize the results in Table

2
.

\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{4,(0)}
SRE Phases

\left[\mathbb{Z}_{2,(0)},\,\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}\right]^{\epsilon}
Gapped Phases

Description

Description

\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2,(1)}
w/

\theta=\pi\,\ell_{4}
, SF=
\ell_{4}

\left[\mathbb{Z}_{2,(0)},\,\ell_{2}\right]

{\rm SPT}(\ell_{2})+{\rm PSB}

\left[{\rm Triv.},\,\ell_{2}\right]

Emergent
\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2,(1)},\,\ell_{2}\right)

+ SSB

\left[{\rm Triv.},\,0\right]
