:

Summary of dualities between

\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{4,(1)}
-symmetric models
with gapped ground states
and
\left[\mathbb{Z}_{2,(0)},\,\mathbb{Z}_{2,(d-2=1)}\right]^{\epsilon_{2}}
-symmetric models with
gapped ground states in
d=3
space dimensions.
We consider gapped ground states of

\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{4,(1)}
-symmetric
Hamiltonians that preserve the subgroup

[\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{p,(1)}]
with
p=1,2,4
.
Such phases have an emergent
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{4/p,(2)}

symmetry which is generated by closed surface loop operators.
The dual models are obtained by gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{4,(1)}

subgroup of the global symmetry.
On the dual side, a phase preserving the
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}
subgroup is mapped
to a phase where dual
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2,(d-2=1)}
symmetry is broken, and
the converse also holds.
The remaining
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}
global symmetry is either broken or
preserved on both sides of the duality.
There is a mixed anomaly between the
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2,(1)}
dual symmetry and
the remaining
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}
global symmetry.
Therefore, on the dual side a gapped phase
that is symmetric under
\left[\mathbb{Z}_{2,(0)},\,\mathbb{Z}_{2,(d-2=1)}\right]^{\epsilon_{2}}
cannot be realized.
When
p=2,4
, the topologically ordered ground state manifold supports emergent

\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2,(2)}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2,(2)}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2,(2)}

symmetries, respectively.
We refer by
``\rm Triv.^{\prime\prime}
to trivial symmetry group.

Let us study the duality in
d=2
or 3 dimensional spin models arising from partial gauging of
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{4,(1)}
.
After such a gauging, one obtains a spin model with a
\mathsf{G}^{\epsilon_{2}}_{(1,d-2)}
symmetry, i.e., a
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2,(d-2)}
global symmetry with a mixed anomaly.
For brevity, we only consider the simplest gapped phases in the spin system with
\mathbb{Z}_{4,(1)}
symmetry, i.e., those corresponding to symmetry breaking of the 1-form symmetry to
\mathbb{Z}_{p,(1)}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{4,(1)}
.
We simply consider the fixed-point Hamiltonians in each gapped phase and obtain the dual partially gauged Hamiltonians by isomorphism of bond algebras between (
6.4
) and (
6.58
).
Such fixed-point Hamiltonians are given in (
6.27
) for
n=4
and
p=1,2,4
.
The results are summarized in Table
6
for
d=3
space dimensions.
The Hamiltonian corresponding to no symmetry breaking and its dual under partial gauging are

\mathcal{H}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{4}]}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{e}}X_{\mathsf{e}}+{\rm H.c}\longmapsto\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{4}]^{\vee}}=-\sum_{\mathsf{e}}\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{e}}\frac{1+A_{\mathsf{e}}}{2}\,.

(6.59)

The ground states of
\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{4}]^{\vee}}
are eigenvalue
+1
states of
A_{\mathsf{e}}
and
\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{e}}
for all
\mathsf{e}
.
This implies that the 1-form symmetry

\mathcal{U}_{(1)}(\Sigma^{(d-1),\vee})=\prod_{\mathsf{e}\subset\Sigma^{(d-1),\vee}}\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{e}}\left[\bar{P}^{(+)}_{\mathsf{e}}+\bar{P}^{(-)}_{\mathsf{e}}A_{\mathsf{e}}\right]\,,

(6.60)

acts as the identity on the ground states and therefore it is preserved.
Let us now consider a product of
A_{\mathsf{e}}
operators taken along an open line
L
in
d=2
or along an open disc
D_{2}
in
d=3
on the dual lattice.
In
d=2
, such a product delivers a bi-local operator
\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{i}(L)}\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{f}(L)}
with
\mathsf{i}(L)
and
\mathsf{f}(L)
being the plaquettes at the two ends of
L
.
Since
\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{p}}
is charged under the 0-form dual symmetry
\mathcal{U}_{(d-2)}
, this signals the spontaneous breaking of the 0-form dual symmetry.
Similarly, in
d=3
, one obtains a line operator

\mathcal{W}(\partial D_{2}^{\vee})=\prod_{\mathsf{e}\in D_{2}^{\vee}}A_{\mathsf{e}}=\prod_{\mathsf{p}\in\partial D_{2}^{\vee}}\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{p}}\,.

(6.61)

The line
\mathcal{W}
is topological in the low energy subspace in the sense that it commutes with the Hamiltonian and therefore does not cost any energy to deform and is charged under the
(d-2=1)
-form symmetry.
Therefore,
\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{4}]^{\vee}}
preserves
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}
while it breaks
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}
dual symmetry.

L
D^{\vee}_{2}

Figure 19
:
In
d=2
, a product of
A_{\mathsf{e}}
operators along a line on the dual lattice furnishes a bi-local operator with support on the two endpoints of the line.
Similarly, in
d=3
, a product of
A_{\mathsf{e}}
operators on an open disc
D_{2}^{\vee}
in the dual lattice furnishes a line operator
\mathcal{W}
on the boundary of the disc.

Next, we consider the partial symmetry breaking
Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}
which dualizes as

\mathcal{H}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]}=-\sum_{\mathsf{e}}X^{2}_{\mathsf{e}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{p}}B_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}\longmapsto\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{\vee}}=-\sum_{\mathsf{e}}A_{\mathsf{e}}-\sum_{\mathsf{p}}\bar{B}_{\mathsf{p}}\,.

(6.62)

Since the ground states of
\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{\vee}}
are eigenvalue
+1
states of
A_{\mathsf{e}}
, it follows due to the above reasoning that
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(d-2)}

dual symmetry is spontaneously broken.
Similarly, the fact the
\bar{B}_{\mathsf{p}}
has eigenvalue +1 on the ground state subspace implies that one may consider an open disc on the direct lattice, which furnishes a line of
\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{e}}
operators on the boundary of this disc.
This line operator has a unit expectation value in the ground state of the fixed-point Hamiltonian but more generally a non-vanishing expectation value anywhere in the gapped phase labelled as
[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{\vee}
.
Finally noting that this line operator is charged under
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}
, implies that the 1-form symmetry is spontaneously broken.
To summarize, we find that in the gapped phase
[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]^{\vee}
the full symmetry
[\mathbb{Z}_{2,(d-2)},\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}]
is broken.

Finally, we move onto the
\mathbb{Z}_{4,(1)}
symmetric gapped phase labelled as
\mathbb{Z}_{1,(1)}
where the 1-form symmetry is completely broken.
The fixed-point Hamiltonian and its dual under partial-gauging have the form

\mathcal{H}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{1}]}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{p}}B_{\mathsf{p}}+{\rm{H.c}}\longmapsto\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{[\mathbb{Z}_{1}]^{\vee}}=-\sum_{\mathsf{p}}\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{p}}\frac{1+\bar{B_{p}}}{2}\,.

(6.63)

This Hamiltonian breaks
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(1)}
since its ground states are in the
\bar{B}_{\mathsf{p}}=1
eigenspace.
It however preserves the
\mathbb{Z}_{2,(d-2)}
dual symmetry as can be readily confirmed.

7
Conclusion

In this paper, we explored various symmetry aspects of quantum spin models models with global higher-form finite Abelian symmetries on arbitrary
d
-dimensional lattices.
Given a
p
-form symmetry corresponding to a finite Abelian group
\mathsf{G}
, we described (i) a systematic gauging of the group
\mathsf{G}
or
any subgroup
\mathsf{H}\subset\mathsf{G}
for
p=0,1
, (ii) the gauging related duality maps between models with a

\mathsf{G}

p
-form symmetry and
\mathsf{G}_{(p,d-p-1)}
higher group symmetry which has a
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{H}

p
-form symmetry, a
\mathsf{H}^{\vee}
(
d-p-1
)-form symmetry and a mixed ’t-Hooft anomaly between these two higher-form symmetries and (iii) dualities between phase diagrams of spin models with the corresponding symmetries.

In Sec.
2
,
we detailed how the gauging of finite Abelian 0-form (sub)-symmetries can be understood as an isomorphism between a bond algebra symmetric with respect to
\mathsf{G}^{\,}_{(0)}
and a dual bond algebra symmetric under the dual
\mathsf{G}_{(0,d-1)}
symmetry.
In particular, we described how the symmetry sectors, i.e., twisted-boundary conditions and symmetry eigenvalue sectors, map under such an isomorphism of bond algebras.
For the case of gauging a subgroup,
we clarified how the mixed anomaly manifests in the symmetry structure of the dual bond algebra.
In doing so, we clarified some anomaly-related subtle symmetry fractionalization patterns of higher-form symmetries in lattice spin models.
In Sec.
3
,
we discussed these gauging-related dualities from a quantum field theory perspective.

In Secs.
4
and
5
, we explored consequences of such gauging related dualities to phase diagrams of two and three dimensional spin models respectively.
We specialized to
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{n}
clock models with

\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{n,(0)}
symmetry and studied a Hamiltonian built as a linear combination of fixed-point Hamiltonians, one for each
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{n,(0)}
-symmetric short-range entangled gapped phase.
By dualizing such a Hamiltonian, using the bond algebra isomorphism obtained in Sec.
2
, we could study various aspects of the phase diagram.
In particular, we could pin-point how all these gapped phases dualize and how certain unconventional (beyond Landau) transitions are dual to Landau transitions under such gaugings.
These studies have potential applications in understanding aspects of such exotic transitions in quantum spin models with global categorical symmetries, a subject about which little is understood.
In Sec.
6
, we studied the gauging of
\mathbb{Z}_{n,(1)}
sub-symmetries in two and three spatial dimensions and applied the corresponding gauging related isomorphisms to spin models with such symmetries.
Among other findings, we showed that in
d=3
, a gauging of
\mathbb{Z}_{n,(1)}
symmetry is realized as an automorphism on the symmetric bond algebra.
This automorphism implied dualities between three-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{n/k}
topological orders and Hamiltonians self-dual under such automorphisms host emergent non-invertible symmetry structures
[
25
]
.

Appendix A
BF type description of bond algebra

In this Appendix, we present an alternative description of
the gauging procedure presented in Sec.
2
as a BF-like theory of
compact scalar fields.
We may represent
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
clock operators as

Z_{\mathsf{v}}\sim e^{{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{v}}}\,,\quad X_{\mathsf{v}}\sim e^{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}}\,,

(A.1)

which satisfy the commutation relations
[\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}},{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}]=2\pi{\rm i}\delta_{\mathsf{v}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}/n\mod 2\pi
.
Here we can think of
\Phi_{\mathsf{v}}
as a compact scalar field and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}
as its canonical momentum operator.
Comparing with (
2.3
) and (
2.5
), we may write the
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetry operator as

\mathcal{U}=\prod_{\mathsf{v}}e^{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}}\,,

(A.2)

and the bond algebra takes the form

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbb{Z}_{n,(0)}}=\Big\langle e^{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}}\,,e^{-{\rm i}\int_{\mathsf{e}}{\rm d}\Phi}\ \Big|\ \forall\ \mathsf{v}\,,\mathsf{e}\Big\rangle\,,

(A.3)

where
e^{-{\rm i}\int_{\mathsf{e}}{\rm d}\Phi}=e^{{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e})}}e^{-{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}}=Z_{\mathsf{(}\mathsf{e})}Z^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})}
.
The local Hilbert space on each vertex is
n
-dimensional such that tensor product Hilbert space is spanned by states labelled by
\phi\in C^{0}(M_{d,\triangle},\mathbb{Z}_{n})
, where
\phi=\left\{\phi_{\mathsf{v}}\right\}_{\mathsf{v}}
with
\phi_{\mathsf{v}}=0,1,\dots,n-1
.
We choose to work in the eigenbasis of
e^{{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{v}}}
such that

\begin{split}e^{{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{v}}}|\phi\rangle&=\omega_{n}^{\phi}|\phi\rangle\,,\\
e^{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}}|\phi\rangle&=|\phi+\delta^{(\mathsf{v})}\rangle\,,\end{split}

(A.4)

where
+
denotes addition modulo
n
and
\delta^{(\mathsf{v})}
is a 0-cochain which evaluates to 1 on the vertex
\mathsf{v}
and
0
elsewhere, i.e.,
\delta^{(\mathsf{v})}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\mathsf{v},\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}
.

In order to gauge the
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
global symmetry, we similarly introduce a
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
degree of freedom on each edge of the lattice.
We denote the operators acting on the edges as
e^{{\rm i}A_{\mathsf{e}}}
and
e^{{\rm i}B_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee}}}
, via the identification

Z_{\mathsf{e}}\sim e^{{\rm i}A_{\mathsf{e}}}\,,\quad X_{\mathsf{e}}\sim e^{{\rm i}B_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee}}}\,.

(A.5)

Note that the
B
operators are defined on
\mathsf{e}^{\vee}
which are
(d-1)
-cells of the dual lattice.
Since, there is a canonical bijection between these
(d-1)
-cells of the dual lattice and the 1-cells of the direct lattice (see, for instance, Fig.
5
) it is always possible to do so. These operators satisfy the commutation relations

\left[B_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee}},A_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}\right]=\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\,\delta_{\mathsf{e},\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}}{n}=\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\,{\rm Int}_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee},\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}}{n}\,,

(A.6)

where in the final expression,
{\rm{Int}}_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee},\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}
is the intersection number of the
(d-1)
-cell
\mathsf{e}^{\vee}
with the edge
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
.
Upon introducing edge degrees of freedom, we span the Hilbert space by basis states
|a,\phi\rangle
, labelled by
a\in C^{1}(M_{d,\triangle},\mathbb{Z}_{n})
and
\phi\in C^{0}(M_{d,\triangle},\mathbb{Z}_{n})
. The vertex operators act on the basis states as (
A.4
), while the edge operators act similarly as

\begin{split}e^{{\rm i}A_{\mathsf{e}}}|a,\phi\rangle&=\omega_{n}^{a_{\mathsf{e}}}|a,\phi\rangle\,,\\
e^{{\rm i}B_{\mathsf{e}^{\vee}}}|a,\phi\rangle&=|a+\delta^{(\mathsf{e})},\phi\rangle\,,\\
\end{split}

(A.7)

where
\delta^{(\mathsf{e})}
is a 1-cochain which evaluates to 1 on the edge
\mathsf{e}
and
0
elsewhere, i.e.,
\delta^{(\mathsf{e})}_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\mathsf{e},\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}
. After gauging, the physical Hilbert space is the gauge invariant subspace of the full Hilbert space spanned by
\left\{|a,\phi\rangle\right\}
.
The gauge invariant subspace is obtained as the identity eigen-sector of the Gauss operator

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{v}}=\exp\left\{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}+{\rm i}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\,,

(A.8)

where
S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}
is a minimal
(d-1)
-sphere on the dual lattice that links with the vertex
\mathsf{v}
(see Fig.
14
). This a field theory notation version of equation (
2.22
), which was written in a more spin-model language.

A general gauge transformation is implemented by the operator
\mathcal{G}[\lambda]=\prod_{\mathsf{v}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{v}}^{\lambda_{\mathsf{v}}}
parametrized by a 0-cochain
\lambda\in C^{0}(M_{d,\triangle},\mathbb{Z}_{n})
which acts on the basis states as

\mathcal{G}[\lambda]|a,\phi\rangle=|a+{\rm d}\lambda,\phi+\lambda\rangle\,.

(A.9)

The bond algebra needs to be suitably modified post gauging such that all the operators are gauge invariant.
We can do so by minimal coupling
{\rm d}\Phi\longrightarrow{\rm d}\Phi+A
, such that bond algebra becomes

\mathfrak{B}_{\mathbb{Z}_{n,(d-1)}}=\Big\langle e^{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}}\,,e^{-{\rm i}\int_{\mathsf{e}}({\rm d}\Phi+A)}\ \Big|\ e^{{\rm i}\oint_{L}A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1\,,\ \mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{v}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1\ \forall\ \mathsf{v}\,,\mathsf{e}\,,L\Big\rangle\,.

(A.10)

There is an additional constraint
\exp\left\{{\rm i}\oint_{L}A\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1
for each loop
L
on the lattice.
This follows from the fact that this operator is the image of the operator
\exp\left\{{\rm i}\oint_{L}\rm d\Phi\right\}=1
in the pre-gauged algbera.
Since gauging is a bond algebra isomorphism, it must map the identity operator to the identity operator. Compare with (
2.27
).
A consequence of this is the fact that
{\rm d}a=0
mod
n
and therefore
a\in Z^{1}(M_{d,\triangle},\mathbb{Z}_{n})
and corresponds to a
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
-valued field.

Next, we seek a unitary transformation that disentangles the edges from the Gauss constraint, i.e.,

\mathcal{U}\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{v}}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}=e^{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}}\,.

(A.11)

Such a transformation is achieved by the unitary

\mathcal{U}=\sum_{a,\phi}|a+{\rm{d}}\phi,\phi\rangle\langle a,\phi|=\prod_{\mathsf{v}}\exp\left\{{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{v}}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\,,

(A.12)

which acts on the remaining operators as

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}\exp\left\{{\rm i}A_{\mathsf{e}}\right\}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}&=\exp\left\{{\rm i}(A+{\rm{d}}\Phi)_{\mathsf{e}}\right\}\,,\\
\mathcal{U}\exp\left\{{\rm i}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}&=\exp\left\{{\rm i}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\,,\\
\mathcal{U}\exp\left\{{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{v}}\right\}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}&=\exp\left\{{\rm i}\Phi_{\mathsf{v}}\right\}\,,\\
\mathcal{U}\exp\left\{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}\right\}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}&=\exp\left\{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}+{\rm i}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\,.\end{split}

(A.13)

The bond algebra (
A.10
) becomes the following after the action of the unitary
\mathcal{U}

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{n,(d-1)}}&=\Big\langle e^{-{\rm i}A_{\mathsf{e}}}\,,\ \exp\left\{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}-{\rm i}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\ \Big|\ e^{{\rm i}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathsf{v}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1\,,\ e^{{\rm i}\oint_{L}A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1\,,\ \forall\ \mathsf{v}\,,\mathsf{e}\,,L\Big\rangle,\\
&=\Big\langle\exp\left\{-{\rm i}A_{\mathsf{e}}\right\}\,,\ \exp\left\{-{\rm i}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\ \Big|\ e^{{\rm i}\oint_{L}A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1\,,\ \forall\ \mathsf{v}\,,\mathsf{e}\,,L\Big\rangle\,.\end{split}

(A.14)

In the second line we have solved the Gauss constraint and frozen out all scalar field d.o.f., the dual bond algebra is organized by conjugate fields
A
and
B
reminiscent of BF-theories. This is the bond algebra of a
(d-1)
-form
\mathbb{Z}_{n,(d-1)}
symmetry generated by closed loops operators

W_{L}=\exp\left(-{\rm i}\int_{L}A\right),

(A.15)

compare this to equation (
2.38
). Similar to the discussion in section
2.1.3
, let us drop the constraint on contractible loops and define the bond algebra

\hat{\mathfrak{B}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{n,(d-1)}}=\Big\langle\exp\left\{-{\rm i}A_{\mathsf{e}}\right\}\,,\ \exp\left\{-{\rm i}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\ \Big|\ \forall\ \mathsf{v}\,,\mathsf{e}\,,L\Big\rangle\,.

(A.16)

Similar to the discussion in section
2.1.3
, we can extend the duality to this larger algebra but the other side will contain algebras with twist defects. Consider the commutative subalgebra

\hat{\mathfrak{B}}_{\left[\mathbb{Z}_{n,(d-1)},\mathbb{Z}_{n,(1)}\right]}=\Big\langle\exp\left\{-{\rm i}\oint_{L}A\right\}\,,\ \exp\left\{-{\rm i}\oint_{S^{(d-1),\vee}_{\mathsf{v}}}B\right\}\ \Big|\ \forall\ \mathsf{v}\,,\mathsf{e}\,,L\Big\rangle\,\subset\hat{\mathfrak{B}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{n,(d-1)}}.

(A.17)

This subalgebra has an extra emergent
1
-form symmetry, generated by
(d-1)
-dimensional submanifolds

\Gamma({S^{(d-1)}}^{\vee})=\exp\left(-{\rm i}\oint_{{S^{(d-1)}}^{\vee}}B\right).

(A.18)

Compare these to (
2.40
) and (
2.42
). Similar to (
2.43
), we can write the
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
toric code Hamiltonian as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} H&=-\sum_{\mathsf{v}}e^{-{\rm i}\oint_{{S^{d-1}_{\mathsf{v}}}^{\vee}}B}-\sum_{\mathsf{p}}e^{-{\rm i}\oint_{L_{\mathsf{p}}}A}+{\rm H.c.}\\
&=-\sum_{\mathsf{v}}e^{-{\rm i}\int_{{X^{d}}_{\mathsf{v}}^{\vee}}H}-\sum_{\mathsf{p}}e^{-{\rm i}\int_{D_{\mathsf{p}}}F}+{\rm H.c.},\end{aligned}

(A.19)

where
{S^{d-1}_{\mathsf{v}}}^{\vee}
is a
d-1
dimensional sphere in the dual lattice wrapping around the vertex
\mathsf{v}
,
L_{\mathsf{p}}
is the curve around a plaquette
\mathsf{p}
, and
{X^{d}}_{\mathsf{v}}^{\vee}
is solid
d
-dim ball such that
\partial{X^{d}}_{\mathsf{v}}^{\vee}={S^{d-1}_{\mathsf{v}}}^{\vee}
and
D_{\mathsf{p}}
is a disk such that
\partial D_{\mathsf{p}}=L_{\mathsf{p}}
. In the second line we used Stokes theorem and defined the
2
- and
d
-form Field strength operators

F={\rm d}A,\qquad H={\rm d}B.

(A.20)

The ground state subspace of toric code requires

F=0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad B=0,

(A.21)

in other words that we have flat
A
and
B
connections. This is exactly the Hilbert space of the underlying TQFT, a
BF
-theory with the action

S=\frac{n}{2\pi}\int_{M}B\wedge dA.

(A.22)

Appendix B
Parallel transport operator for
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
spin-chains

A
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetric spin chain can be coupled to a background gauge field, also called a
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
connection. This allows us to define parallel transport on the space of operators. In this appendix we construct the parallel transport operator explicitly in terms of spin operators. This construction works in any dimension and on any triangulation, no need for translation symmetry.

Consider a triangulation
M_{d,\triangle}
of an oriented
d
dimensional manifold
M_{d}
. There is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n}
associated to each vertex
\mathsf{v}
of
M_{d,\triangle}
, with the total Hilbert space
\mathcal{V}=\bigotimes_{\mathsf{v}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}
. All linear operators on
\mathcal{V}
are generated by
X_{\mathsf{v}}
and
Z_{\mathsf{v}}
with the algebra

Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{\alpha}X_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}^{\mathsf{g}}=\omega_{n}^{\alpha\mathsf{g}\,\delta_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}}X_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}^{\mathsf{g}}Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{\alpha}.

(B.1)

We want to construct a permutation operator
P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}
with the property

P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{v}}P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}^{-1}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}},

(B.2)

written in terms of the operators
X_{\mathsf{v}}
and
Z_{\mathsf{v}}
. Here
P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}
permutes local operators acting only on the vertices
\mathsf{v}
and
\overline{\mathsf{v}}
, while commuting with any other local operators. The space of linear invertible operators at each vertex

GL\big(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}})\simeq\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\Big\{X_{\mathsf{v}}^{\mathsf{g}}Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{\alpha}\;\Big|\;\mathsf{g},\alpha=0,\dots,n-1\Big\},

(B.3)

can be endowed with the inner product

\langle A,B\rangle=\frac{1}{n}\tr_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}}\left[A^{\dagger}B\right],\qquad A,B\in GL(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}).

(B.4)

A short calculation shows that with this inner product, the above chosen basis is orthonormal

\langle X_{\mathsf{v}}^{\mathsf{g}}Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{\alpha},X_{\mathsf{v}}^{\overline{\mathsf{g}}}Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{\overline{\alpha}}\rangle=\delta_{\mathsf{g},\overline{\mathsf{g}}}\delta_{\alpha,\overline{\alpha}}.

(B.5)

We can extend this basis to all of
GL(\mathcal{V})\equiv\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
, by using the standard isomorphism

GL\left(\bigotimes_{\mathsf{v}}\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}\right)\simeq\bigotimes_{\mathsf{v}}GL\left(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{v}}\right),

(B.6)

where on
\mathcal{V}
we normalize the inner product as

\langle A,B\rangle=\frac{1}{n^{\#\text{vertices}}}\tr_{\mathcal{V}}\left[A^{\dagger}B\right],\qquad A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V}).

(B.7)

Using the eigenbasis of the
Z_{\mathsf{v}}
operators

Z_{\mathsf{v}}|\phi\rangle=\omega_{n}^{\phi_{\mathsf{v}}}|\phi\rangle,

(B.8)

we can explicitly construct the permutation operator as

P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}=\sum_{\phi}|S_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}\cdot\phi\rangle\langle\phi|,

(B.9)

where
S_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}
acting on the labels
\phi=\{\phi_{\mathsf{v}}\}
, swaps
\phi_{\mathsf{v}}
and
\phi{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}
. One can easily see that following properties

P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}|\dots,\phi_{\mathsf{v}},\dots,\phi_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}},\dots\rangle=|\dots,\phi_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}},\dots,\phi_{\mathsf{v}},\dots\rangle,\qquad P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}^{2}=1,

(B.10)

as wanted. In order to write this operator in terms of the aforementioned basis of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
, let us consider a general superposition

P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}=\sum_{\mathsf{g},\alpha=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\overline{\mathsf{g}},\overline{\alpha}=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{M}^{\overline{\mathsf{g}},\overline{\alpha}}_{\mathsf{g},\alpha}\;X^{\mathsf{g}}_{\mathsf{v}}Z^{\alpha}_{\mathsf{v}}X^{\overline{\mathsf{g}}}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}Z^{\overline{\alpha}}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}.

(B.11)

We are only expanding this in the subspace of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{V})
corresponding to operators that only act on the vertices
\mathsf{v}
and
\overline{\mathsf{v}}
, since it must commute with any operator
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}
such that
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\not=\mathsf{v}
or
\overline{\mathsf{v}}
. The coefficients can be computed using the inner product (
B.7
) and the orthonormality of our basis

\left\langle\prod_{\mathsf{v}}X_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}}Z_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}},P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}\right\rangle=\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{v}},\alpha_{\mathsf{v}}}_{\mathsf{g}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}},\alpha_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}}\prod_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\not=\mathsf{v},\overline{\mathsf{v}}}\delta_{\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}},\overline{\mathsf{g}}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}}\delta_{\alpha_{\mathsf{v}},\overline{\alpha}_{\mathsf{v}}}.

(B.12)

In particular, using (
B.9
) and only the basis vectors of the relevant two-body subspace, one can show by a calculation that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{v}},\alpha_{\mathsf{v}}}_{\mathsf{g}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}},\alpha_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}}

\displaystyle=\langle X_{\mathsf{v}}^{\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{v}}}Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{\alpha_{\mathsf{v}}}X_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}^{\mathsf{g}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}}Z_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}^{\alpha_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}},P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}\rangle,

(B.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\omega^{\mathsf{g}_{v}\alpha_{v}}\;\delta_{\alpha_{\mathsf{v}}+\alpha_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}},0}\,\delta_{\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{v}}+\mathsf{g}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}},0}.

(B.14)

We can therefore write
P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}
in the form we wanted

P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\mathsf{g},\alpha=0}^{n-1}\omega^{\mathsf{g}\alpha}\;X_{\mathsf{v}}^{\mathsf{g}}Z_{\mathsf{v}}^{\alpha}\,X_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}^{-\mathsf{g}}Z_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}^{-\alpha}.

(B.16)

One can readily check that this satisfies all the needed properties such as (
B.2
). For a standard spin-model with
n=2
we get

\displaystyle P_{\mathsf{v}\overline{\mathsf{v}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\bm{1}+X_{\mathsf{v}}\otimes X_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}-(XZ)_{\mathsf{v}}\otimes(XZ)_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}+Z_{\mathsf{v}}\otimes Z_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}\Big]

(B.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\bm{1}+\sigma^{x}_{\mathsf{v}}\otimes\sigma^{x}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}+\sigma^{y}_{\mathsf{v}}\otimes\sigma^{y}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}+\sigma^{z}_{\mathsf{v}}\otimes\sigma^{z}_{\overline{\mathsf{v}}}\Big].

(B.18)

For each edge
\mathsf{e}
, we can define

T_{\mathsf{e}}=P_{\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{e}),\mathsf{t}(\mathsf{e})},

(B.19)

where
s(\mathsf{e})
and
t(\mathsf{e})
is the source and target of
\mathsf{e}
, respectively.

For regular lattices, this can be used to construct translation operators in terms of spin operators. For example for a one-dimensional lattice of
L
sites, we have

T=\prod_{i=1}^{L-1}P_{i,i+1}=P_{1,2}P_{2,3}\dots P_{L-1,L},

(B.20)

which satisfies

T|\phi_{1}\dots\phi_{L}\rangle=|\phi_{L}\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\dots\phi_{L-1}\rangle.

(B.21)

On general triangulations we can construct Parallel transport operators, when coupling the global symmetry to a background connection (gauge field). See equation (
2.10
) and surrounding discussion.
